## Page 1


LOCAL-GLOBAL COMPATIBILITY OF AUTOMORPHIC
GALOIS REPRESENTATIONS OVER CM FIELDS AT p
by
L. A’Campo, B. Hevesi, J. A. Thorne & D. Whitmore
Abstract. —
Let F be a CM number field; then, to any cuspidal, regular
algebraic automorphic representation of GLn(AF ) is associated a compatible
system of p-adic Galois representations of the absolute Galois group of F.
We prove that these representations are potentially semi-stable, in the sense
of p-adic Hodge theory, and satisfy compatibility with the local Langlands
correspondence, up to semi-simplification.

1. Introduction
1.1. Context. — Let F be a number field, and let n ≥1. According to
the philosophy of the Langlands programme, automorphic representations of
GLn(AF ) should be related to Galois representations, i.e. n-dimensional rep-
resentations of the absolute Galois group GF = Gal(F/F).
A conjectural
answer to the question of which automorphic representations should partic-
ipate in this correspondence was given by Clozel [Clo90], who singled out
those representations that are ‘algebraic’ (a condition depending only on the
infinite component π∞of an automorphic representation π). Clozel made the
following conjecture:
1.1.1. Conjecture. Let π be a cuspidal, algebraic automorphic representation
of GLn(AF ). Then:
1. The finite part π∞(an irreducible admissible representation of GLn(A∞
F ))
is defined over a number field Kπ ≤C.
2. For any prime p and embedding Kπ →Qp, there is a continuous, semi-
simple representation rπ,p : GF →GLn(Qp) with the following property:
for any finite place v ∤p such that πv is unramified, the local representa-
tion rπ,p|GFv is unramified, and rπ,p(Frobv)ss ∈GLn(Qp) is conjugate to
the (Tate–normalised) Satake parameter tT
πv ∈GLn(Qp).
(Here GFv ≤GF denotes the decomposition group, defined up to conjuga-
tion, and Frobv ∈GFv is a choice of geometric Frobenius lift; see §1.4 below
for any further undefined notation.)
The Chebotarev density theorem im-
plies that rπ,p is determined uniquely up to isomorphism by the matrices tT
πv.
Henceforth we usually avoid mentioning Kπ and speak in terms of a choice of

(This is the same as Conjecture 1.1.2, up to ‘forgetting the N’.) Before
coming to our results, we recall what is known about Conjectures 1.1.2 and
1.1.3. As mentioned above, a candidate representation rπ,ι satisfying local-
global compatibility at all but finitely many unramified places has already
been constructed, so the question is really whether this representation satisfies
local-global compatibility at every place. The following results are known:
– If π is conjugate self-dual, then Conjecture 1.1.2 is known to hold in
complete generality (see e.g. [Car14] for the capstone in a long series of
works by many authors on this problem).
– If v ∤p, then Varma proved [Var24] that rπ,ι satisfies semi-simplified
local-global compatibility at v, using a refinement of the ‘p-adic approxi-
mation by overconvergent conjugate self-dual cusp forms’ approach given

LOCAL-GLOBAL COMPATIBILITY
5
in [HLTT16]. This approach is harder to apply when v|p, because p-adic
limits of de Rham Galois representations of GFv need not be de Rham.
– If v|p, then many cases of local-global compatibility at v are known,
provided that rπ,ι satisfies auxiliary global hypotheses, such as that the
residual representation rπ,ι be irreducible, and that there exist a ‘de-
composed generic’ prime ℓ̸= p, such that at places w|ℓof F, rπ,ι|GFw
is unramified, with Frobenius eigenvalues that are ‘as generic as possi-
ble’. As a representative example, we cite e.g. [Hev24, Theorem 1.1],
which proves Conjecture 1.1.3 under conditions of this type, using an
elaboration of the degree-shifting argument introduced in [ACC+23].
Let U(n, n) denote the quasi-split unitary group in 2n variables associated to
the quadratic extension F/F + (of F over its maximal totally real subfield).
The arithmetic locally symmetric spaces associated to U(n, n) (or rather, their
Borel–Serre compactifications) admit boundary components corresponding to
parabolic subgroups, in particular to the Siegel parabolic, with Levi GLn/F .
The global conditions mentioned in the previous paragraph greatly simplify
the analysis of the cohomology of the U(n, n) symmetric space with integral
(say, Zp or Z/pNZ) coefficients.
For example, the ‘non-Eisenstein’ (or irreducibility of residual representa-
tion) condition implies that no boundary components contribute to the co-
homology groups, after localisation at a suitable maximal ideal of the Hecke
algebra, except those corresponding to the Siegel parabolic. The ‘decomposed
generic’ condition implies that one can invoke the vanishing results of [CS24],
which are the key input into the degree-shifting argument of [ACC+23]: they
imply that, for suitable Zp-free local systems Vλ, middle degree cohomology
classes with coefficients in Vλ/(pN) can always be lifted to middle degree classes
with coefficients in Vλ. This allows the construction of congruences with cusp-
idal, regular algebraic, conjugate self-dual automorphic forms for GL2n(AF ),
with controlled Hodge–Tate weights at p-adic embeddings τ lying above v.
1.2. Results of this paper. — We complete the proof of Conjecture 1.1.3
for CM number fields F, by establishing semi-simplified local-global compati-
bility at the p-adic places:
1.2.1. Theorem. Let F be a CM number field, let n ≥1, and let ι: Qp →
C be an isomorphism.
Let π be a cuspidal, regular algebraic automorphic
representation of GLn(AF ) of weight λ. Then for any place v|p of F, the
representation rπ,ι|GFv is de Rham of Hodge–Tate weights
HTτ(rπ,ι) = {λιτ,1 + (n −1), λιτ,2 + (n −2), . . . , λιτ,n},
and there is an isomorphism WD(rπ,ι|GFv )ss ∼= ι−1 recT
Fv(πv)ss.
In fact, Theorem 1.2.1 has the following apparently stronger corollary:

6
L. A’CAMPO, B. HEVESI, J. A. THORNE & D. WHITMORE
1.2.2. Corollary. In the situation of Theorem 1.2.1, we have
WD(rπ,ι|GFv )F-ss ≺ι−1 recT
Fv(πv).
If (r, N) and (r′, N′) are Frobenius-semisimple Weil–Deligne representa-
tions, the relation (r, N) ≺(r′, N′), defined in [Var24, §8], means that N′
is ‘closer to regular nilpotent than N’. We recall the definition of this rela-
tion, and explain why Theorem 1.2.1 implies Corollary 1.2.2, in §6 below.
We now sketch the proof of Theorem 1.2.1.
The basic idea is to carry
out the degree-shifting argument of [ACC+23] without any additional global
hypotheses.
We first need to find a replacement for the torsion-vanishing
results of [CS24], which state that, after localisation at a decomposed generic
maximal ideal of the Hecke algebra, the cohomology of the locally symmetric
space of U(n, n) vanishes below the middle degree d. (There is a dual statement
for compactly supported cohomology, which follows by Poincar´e duality.)
1.2.3.
We prove a quantitative version of this statement. More precisely, if w
is an unramified place, split over the maximal totally real subfield of F, then
we construct an unramified Hecke operator Tw with the following properties:
– The eigenvalue of Tw in any generic unramified representation of
GL2n(Fw) over C is non-zero.
– There is an explicit integer N = N(n, F, w) ≥0 such that T N
w annihilates
the degree i cohomology of the locally symmetric space for all i < d.
See Theorem 2.5.7 for a precise statement. This implies the main theorem
of [CS24] in the case that p is a banal characteristic for GL2n(Fw), since
Tw then becomes a unit after localisation at a generic maximal ideal. It is
also well-adapted to our target theorem, because any cuspidal automorphic
representation of GLn has the property that all of its local components are
generic [Sha74]. The proof of Theorem 2.5.7 follows the strategy introduced
in [CS24].
More precisely, our argument follows the variant developed in
[Kos21]: rather than analysing the cohomology of Igusa varieties, we study
the cohomology of local Shimura varieties appearing in Mantovan’s product
formula, using the machinery of [FS24].
1.2.4.
We next need to account for the removal of the non-Eisenstein con-
dition. This creates two difficulties. First, on the Galois side: if the residual
representation rπ,ι is irreducible, then it has a well-defined deformation ring,
and we can write down quotients corresponding to conditions from p-adic
Hodge theory (such as ‘crystalline with given Hodge–Tate weights’) using the
results of [Kis08]. This shows in particular that, if rπ,ι can be presented as
a p-adic limit of Galois representations satisfying such conditions, then it will
also satisfy these conditions.


## Page 7


LOCAL-GLOBAL COMPATIBILITY
7
If rπ,ι is reducible, then we need to deal with pseudocharacters and their de-
formations in the sense of [Che14]. These admit a pseudodeformation space,
but the question of imposing conditions from p-adic Hodge theory, studied
e.g. in [WE15], is more subtle. For example, in op. cit. pseudodeformation
spaces with p-adic Hodge–theoretic conditions are constructed when rπ,ι is
residually multiplicity-free; but we want a result with no condition at all on
the residual representation. The key new observation we make here is that
the finiteness of cohomology of reductive groups over Noetherian rings can be
combined with the ideas developed in [WE15], and the theory of ‘pseudode-
formations with conditions’ developed in [WWE19], to construct the needed
pseudodeformation spaces. This construction is the subject of §3.
1.2.5.
The second difficulty that arises without the non-Eisenstein condition
is that, in considering the cohomology of the (Borel–Serre compactification of
the) locally symmetric space for U(n, n), we need to consider contributions
from parabolic subgroups other than the Siegel parabolic. Indeed, it is the
cohomology of the whole Borel–Serre boundary that admits comparison with
the cohomology of the U(n, n) locally symmetric space (via the excision ex-
act sequence); in the non-Eisenstein case, the cohomology of the Borel–Serre
boundary and the cohomology of the Siegel component agree, after localisa-
tion, but otherwise the best one can hope for is that the interior cohomology
of the Siegel component is a subquotient of the cohomology of the Borel–Serre
boundary.
As interior cohomology, being defined as the image of the compactly sup-
ported cohomology in the usual cohomology, does not behave well in exact se-
quences, this necessitates a complicated induction argument, where we prove
Theorem 1.2.1 (and in fact, a version for the cohomology with torsion coeffi-
cients of the GLn/F -locally symmetric space) by induction on n. Since we need
to use coefficient systems that pick out automorphic representations with the
right Hodge–Tate weights and inertial type at the place v, this further requires
us to understand what these local systems look like when restricted to bound-
ary components, via an excursion into type theory for the group GLn(Fv).
We recall the needed local results in §4, and carry out this new degree-shifting
argument, including the necessary induction on n, in §5.
1.3. Remarks. — The induction argument in §5 would not be needed if we
knew that the representations rπ,ι were irreducible (as is conjectured to be the
case). On the other hand, the knowledge that the representations rπ,ι satisfy
the expected conditions from p-adic Hodge theory is a useful input into many
proofs of irreducibility (see e.g. [Sha26]).
Although the main theorem of this paper concerns the Qp-representations
associated to automorphic representations, it is also of interest to establish


## Page 8


8
L. A’CAMPO, B. HEVESI, J. A. THORNE & D. WHITMORE
local-global compatibility for torsion classes, with the aim of proving e.g.
R = T theorems under more degenerate conditions than the ones consid-
ered in [ACC+23].
Our arguments do give meaningful results for torsion
classes. However, instead of proving the expected result ‘up to a nilpotent
ideal’, we are able to prove a result ‘up to bounded Tw-torsion’, where Tw is
the operator described above, which is (at least in the banal case) nonzero
modulo any decomposed generic maximal ideal of the Hecke algebra.
We
apply these results to prove the vanishing of the adjoint Bloch–Kato Selmer
group for many automorphic Galois representations in the companion paper
[AHTW26], generalising the theorems proved in [NT23] (which just treated
the conjugate self-dual part of the adjoint Bloch–Kato Selmer group for con-
jugate self-dual automorphic Galois representations).

1.4. Notation. — If F is a field of characteristic zero, we generally fix an
algebraic closure F/F and write GF for the absolute Galois group of F with
respect to this choice. If F is a number field, then we will also fix embeddings
F →F v extending the map F →Fv for each place v of F; this choice deter-
mines a homomorphism GFv →GF . When v is a finite place, we will write
OFv ⊂Fv for the valuation ring, ϖv ∈OFv for a fixed choice of uniformizer,
Frobv ∈GFv for a fixed choice of Frobenius lift, k(v) = OFv/(ϖv) for the
residue field, and qv = #k(v) for the cardinality of the residue field. When v
is a real place, we write cv ∈GFv for complex conjugation.
If p is a prime, then we call a coefficient field a finite extension E/Qp
contained inside our fixed algebraic closure Qp, and write O for the valuation
ring of E, ϖ ∈O for a fixed choice of uniformizer, and k = O/(ϖ) for the
residue field. (In the body of the paper, we generally use p-adic coefficients,
except in §2, where we use ℓ-adic coefficients in order to be consistent with
the relevant literature.)

Let K be a non-archimedean characteristic 0 local field, and let Ωbe an
algebraically closed field of characteristic 0.
We write WK ⊂GK for the
Weil group of K and IK ⊂WK for the inertia subgroup. We write recT
K for
the Tate normalisation of the local Langlands correspondence for GLn(K): it
is the bijection recT
K between isomorphism classes of irreducible, admissible
Ω[GLn(K)]-modules and isomorphism classes of Frobenius-semisimple Weil–
Deligne representations over Ωof rank n defined in [CT14, §2.1]. When Ω=
C, it is related to the usual unitary normalisation by the formula recT
K(π) =
recK(π| · |(1−n)/2).
If K is a p-adic local field, τ : K →Qp is a continuous embedding, and
ρ: GK →GLn(Qp) is a continuous representation, then we write HTτ(ρ) for
the multiset of integers i such that gri(ρ ⊗τ,K BdR)GK ̸= 0, with multiplicity
equal to the dimension of this Qp-vector space. If ρ is de Rham, then this set
has n elements (counted with multiplicity). In particular, if ρ is the p-adic
cyclotomic character, we have HTτ(ρ) = {−1} for all τ.
2. Torsion in the cohomology of Shimura varieties
An essential input in the proof of the local–global compatibility theorems
of [ACC+23] is the main result of [CS24], which establishes the vanishing
of the mod ℓcompactly supported cohomology of ResF +/QGU(n, n)-Shimura
varieties above the middle degree after localisation at decomposed generic
maximal ideals of the global Hecke algebra. Equivalently, this can be formu-
lated as vanishing above the middle degree after inverting a Hecke operator ∆
at an auxiliary prime p ̸= ℓ, which measures the genericity of unramified prin-
cipal series representations. The goal of this section is to prove a quantitative
version of this vanishing result.
In [Kos21], Koshikawa gives a new proof of the vanishing result of [CS24]
using the framework of Fargues–Scholze [FS24].
He first proves that, af-
ter localisation at generic maximal ideals of the local Hecke algebra Hp for
p ̸= ℓ, or equivalently after inverting ∆∈Hp, the mod ℓcompactly supported
cohomology groups of local Shimura varieties associated with non-ordinary
Kottwitz elements vanish. He then deduces the global vanishing result for
unitary Shimura varieties by combining a version of Mantovan’s product for-
mula for cohomology without compact support with the semi-perversity result
of [CS24], §4.6.
Following Koshikawa’s strategy, we combine the description of the coho-
mology of local Shimura varieties provided by the Fargues–Scholze machinery
with the local–local compatibility result of [HKW22]. This allows us to show


## Page 10


10
L. A’CAMPO, B. HEVESI, J. A. THORNE & D. WHITMORE
that the compactly supported cohomology of non-ordinary local Shimura va-
rieties with coefficients in any Zℓ[p1/2]-algebra Λ is annihilated by M∆, for
some integer M ≥1 depending only on n and F.
Finally, we deduce that, above the middle degree, the compactly supported
cohomology of the unitary Shimura variety with coefficients in Λ is annihi-
lated by a power of M∆, with exponent depending only on n and F. For this,
we use Mantovan’s product formula for compactly supported cohomology es-
tablished in [HL25], reducing the problem to the vanishing above the middle
degree of the contribution of the ordinary stratum. This is then obtained from
the corresponding result for partially compactly supported cohomology of the
ordinary Igusa variety, together with cases of the Harris–Viehmann conjecture
proved in [GI22].

In [RR96, §2.3], they describe a partial order ≺on B(H) using the Newton
and Kottwitz maps. We equip B(H) with the topology induced by opposite

µ-admissible elements that forms an open subset with respect to the induced
topology.
For later use, we note that we will write µ−1 for the dominant

For every b ∈B(H), we obtain a reductive group Hb over Qp with functor
of points
Hb(R) = {g ∈H(R ⊗Qp ˇQp) | gb = bσ(g)}.
If H is quasisplit, Hb is an inner form of a Levi subgroup of H. In general, it
is an inner form of the Levi of the quasisplit inner form of H.

3. Potentially semistable pseudodeformation rings
The goal of this section is to define spaces of pseudocharacters, i.e. de-
terminants in the sense of [Che14], satisfying conditions from p-adic Hodge
theory (e.g. potentially semistable with fixed Hodge and inertial type). Wang-
Erickson [WE15] showed how to do this under the condition that the residual
pseudocharacter is multiplicity-free, and also under the (unproved) condition
that the algebraic stack of representations with given pseudocharacter satisfies


## Page 27


LOCAL-GLOBAL COMPATIBILITY
27
formal GAGA. Roughly speaking, the techniques of [Kis08] allow one to de-
fine a closed locus in the associated formal stack of representations satisfying
the given conditions; using formal GAGA, one can algebraise this locus and
take its image in the spectrum of the universal pseudodeformation ring to get
the desired space of pseudocharacters.
We do not wish to put any hypothesis on the residual representation, such
as being multiplicity-free. We get around the need to establish formal GAGA
by combining the approach of [WE15] with the ideas of Wake–Wang-Erickson
[WWE19]. In the latter paper, the authors showed how to define a quotient
of the universal pseudodeformation ring corresponding to conditions such as
‘semistable after passage to L/K, with Hodge–Tate weights in an interval
[a, b]’. Returning to the setting of [WE15], this replaces the problem of al-
gebraising an arbitrary closed formal substack with the much easier one of
algebraising a union of connected components of its generic fibre. We com-
plete the argument here by proving ‘half’ of formal GAGA in our setting,
namely the statement that passage to completion is a fully faithful functor for
certain quotient stacks. This necessitates a detour through certain finiteness
results, that we turn to first.
3.1. Finiteness of cohomology. — Let S be a Noetherian ring and G a
reductive group over S (i.e. a smooth affine group scheme whose geometric
fibres are reductive groups, in particular, connected).
A finitely generated
S-algebra A with G-action corresponds to an affine scheme of finite type over
S with an action of G. We will need the following theorem.
3.1.1. Theorem.
1. AG is a finitely generated S-algebra.
2. If M is a finite A-module with semilinear G-action, then for any n ≥0,
Hn(G, M) is a finite AG-module.
Part (1) is proved as [FvdK10, Theorem 3], using general properties of
the Grosshans filtration, and part (2) is proved as [FvdK10, Theorem 49],
under the additional assumption that S = Z, and as [vdK15, Theorem 10.5],
without any assumption on S. (See also [Alp14, Theorem 6.3.3] for the case
n = 0.) The deduction of [vdK15, Theorem 10.5] uses advanced tools (includ-
ing results of [SFB97]), but in fact part (2) of the theorem can be deduced
from the case n = 0 using a relatively simple dimension-shifting argument.
Since this seems worth recording, we sketch the details here.
By descent, we can assume that G is split. Choose Borel subgroups B, B−≤
G which are opposite with respect to a maximal torus T = B ∩B−≤G. Let
U ≤B, U−≤B−denote the respective unipotent radicals. We take the set
Φ(G, T)+ of positive roots to be those belonging to B. Recall [Gro92, Lemma
6] that there exists a homomorphism h: X•(T) →Z such that
1. for all λ ∈X•(T)+, h(λ) ≥0,


## Page 28


28
L. A’CAMPO, B. HEVESI, J. A. THORNE & D. WHITMORE
2. if λ > µ, then h(λ) > h(µ),
3. for all λ ∈X•(G), we have h(λ) = 0.
For example, if G = GL2 and T ≤B ≤G are the standard diagonal torus and
upper-triangular Borel subgroup, then we can take h(λ1, λ2) = λ1 −λ2.
3.1.2. Definition. For any S-module M with G-action, we define the
Grosshans filtration
M0 ≤M1 ≤· · · ≤M,
where Mn ⊂M is the largest G-stable S-submodule such that every weight
λ ∈X•(T) appearing in Mn satisfies h(λ) ≤n. The associated graded module
is denoted by gr M. It is again an S-module with G-action.
We record the following properties of this filtration (where M, N are S-
modules with G-action, and A is an S-algebra with G-action):
1. The weights appearing in M ⊗S N are sums of weights appearing in M
and N. Therefore, the image of Mi ⊗S Nj in M ⊗S N is contained in
(M ⊗S N)i+j. In particular, if A is an S-algebra with G-action, then
Ai · Aj ≤Ai+j.
2. For a T-module P, let P(i) = ⊕h(λ)=iPλ. Then MU only has dominant
weights, and for any i ≥0, the map (MU)(i) →(gri M)U is an isomor-
phism.
Consequently, there is an isomorphism MU ∼= (gr M)U of S-
modules with T-action. Moreover, the induced map gr M →IndG
B−MU
is injective [FvdK10, Lemma 26].
3. The ring homomorphism gr A →IndG
B−AU is an integral ring extension,
by [FvdK10, Theorem 29].
4. If A is finitely generated over S, then both gr A and IndG
B−AU are finitely
generated S-algebras, by [FvdK10, Theorem 30], and consequently the
map gr A →IndG
B−AU is a finite algebra homomorphism.
3.1.3. Lemma. Let A be a finitely generated S-algebra with G-action and
M a finite A-module with semilinear G-action. Then IndG

gr A-module.

gr M is a finitely generated S-algebra, hence a finite gr A-algebra. Moreover,
IndG
B−(A′)U is a finite gr A′-module, hence also a finite gr A-module.
The
result follows.

3.3. Application to Galois deformations. — In this section we apply
theorem 3.2.4 to the deformation theory of pseudorepresentations of absolute
Galois groups.
In particular, we prove the results of [WE15] without the
multiplicity-free hypothesis. As a consequence we are able to define potentially
semistable Galois pseudodeformations with fixed p-adic Hodge type and Galois
type.

– Ccr
L/K,[a,b]: Subquotient of a Zp-lattice in a de Rham Qp[GK]-module,

3.3.15.
As the Remark shows, it is not possible to prove our main local-
global compatibility theorem by working only with local pseudodeformation
rings with conditions. However, the structure of the degree-shifting argument
means that they must play a role. (Roughly speaking, to study n-dimensional
automorphic Galois representations we need to introduce 2n-dimensional au-
tomorphic Galois representations arising from the unitary group; these do not
have a canonical invariant n-dimensional invariant subspace, but they do after
restriction to decomposition groups at the p-adic places of interest – cf. the
proof of Lemma 5.4.18.)
Ultimately, we are saved by the observation that if we have a p-adic,
semisimple global representation ρ : GF,S →GLn(Qp) that is potentially
semistable, and (ρ|GFv )ss has both the correct Hodge–Tate weights and the
correct inertial type, then ρ|GFv also has the correct Hodge–Tate weights
and correct inertial type.
This observation is formalised in Lemma 3.3.20
and informs the axiomatisation of the induction hypothesis underlying the
degree-shifting argument that we give in Definition 5.2.4.
3.3.16.
Given an Artinian local O-algebra (A, m) with residue field k and a
(continuous) deformation D : GF,S →A of D, a Cayley–Hamilton represen-
tation (E, ρ, D′) lifting D consists of a Cayley–Hamilton pseudorepresentation
D′ : E →A in the sense of [WWE19, Definition 2.1.6] and a (continuous)

a finitely generated A-module and so it is naturally an object of the category
Modfin(Zp[GF,S]) of continuous GF,S-representations on Zp-modules of finite
cardinality.

4. Some local representation theory
This section prepares the proof of Proposition 5.3.2, which describes the
Hecke eigensystems appearing in the Betti cohomology of Borel–Serre strata
of ResF/QGLn-locally symmetric spaces in terms of the cohomology of their
Levi factors.
Understanding the local systems arising in this description reduces to the
study of certain continuous group cohomology groups of Zp-lattices in locally
algebraic types. After inverting p, the smooth part of such a type is given by
a Bushnell–Kutzko type, while the algebraic part is given by a highest weight
representation. The corresponding descriptions are provided by Proposition
4.1.4 and Theorem 4.2.2. In Lemma 4.4.5 and Lemma 4.4.6, we show that, up
to ignoring pN-torsion (with exponent depending only on the choices made in
§4.3), the integral local systems that arise agree with those predicted by the
characteristic 0 formulas of §4.1 and §4.2.
Describing the Hecke action at p is more subtle. To do this, in Lemma 4.1.5
we single out a collection of Hecke operators arising from Bushnell–Kutzko’s
generalisation of Bernstein’s presentation for which such a description is pos-
sible, and in Lemma 4.1.6 we show that these operators suffice to control the
action of the Bernstein centre. In §4.3, we rescale these operators to make
them compatible with the chosen lattices in our locally algebraic types. Fi-
nally, in §4.4, we carry out the necessary computations in the derived category
of smooth p-power torsion representations needed to compare the action of this
collection of Hecke operators with the characteristic 0 formula appearing in
Remark 4.2.4.
4.1. Smooth representations with characteristic 0 coefficients. —
Let L/Qp be a finite field extension, G := GLn(L). For every integer m ≥1

The work of Bushnell–Kutzko [BK93], [BK98], [BK99] allows us to define

5. Local-global compatibility for Betti cohomology
The goal of this final section is to prove Theorem 5.2.9, our main result
on local–global compatibility for automorphic Galois representations. We de-
duce the characteristic 0 statement from Proposition 5.2.8, which establishes
a more general local–global compatibility result for the Betti cohomology of


## Page 66


66
L. A’CAMPO, B. HEVESI, J. A. THORNE & D. WHITMORE
ResF/QGLn-locally symmetric spaces. The proof proceeds by induction on
n ≥1.
The inductive hypothesis is used to establish local–global compatibility for
the contribution of the Borel–Serre boundary.
This is carried out in §5.3
and is the only point where the results of §4 are needed. Our analysis yields a
clean description of the boundary cohomology only after discarding pN-torsion,
where the exponent N depends only on n, F, and the chosen local system.
Consequently, Proposition 5.2.8 is also formulated modulo such pN-torsion.
It remains to treat the interior cohomology, where it suffices to prove lo-
cal–global compatibility for cohomology with coefficients in certain flat Zp-
local systems.(7)
This is achieved by following the strategy of [ACC+23].
More precisely, we employ a slightly modified version of the P-ordinary degree-
shifting argument developed in [CN25]. In the course of the proof, to produce
the required congruences with conjugate self-dual automorphic representa-
tions of GL2n(AF ), we replace the vanishing result of [CS24] with Theorem
2.5.7. As a consequence, we must also ignore (M∆)N-torsion, for some integers
M, N ≥1 depending only on n and F.
5.1. Generalities on locally symmetric spaces. — We introduce some
key notation, following [NT16] and [ACC+23]. If F is a number field, and
G is a connected linear algebraic group over F, we will write XG for the
symmetric space associated by Borel–Serre [BS73] to the group ResF/Q G,
X

Our proof of Proposition 5.2.8 will be broken into two separate parts. First,
we describe the cohomology of the Borel–Serre boundary of XGLn in terms
of “torsion Eisenstein series” with desired local behaviour at our fixed p-adic
place allowing us to show that the boundary cohomology satisfies local-global
compatibility by induction on n. As we have limited control over the local sys-
tems appearing in this analysis, this forces us to ignore pN2-torsion of exponent
depending on our initial local system. This is a new phenomenon compared to
previous works [ACC+23], [AC23], [CN25] and [Hev24] originating in re-
laxing the residual irreducibility condition in their theorems. Indeed, in any of
the listed articles, results are only proved after localisation at a non-Eisenstein
maximal ideal, which has the effect of immediately killing all boundary coho-
mology for XGLn.
Finally, to fill the gap between the boundary cohomology and cohomology
(without compact supports) with O/ϖm-coefficients, it turns out to be suffi-
cient to prove local-global compatibility at a ∈Sp for the interior cohomology
of XGLn with O-coefficients with values in a local system suitably chosen at
places away from a. This puts us into the situation that is handled by the “de-
gree shifting argument” introduced in [ACC+23]. More precisely, we closely
follow the P-ordinary version of the argument as presented in [CN25] and
[Hev24], building on [ACC+23], and [AC23], with the key difference coming
from replacing the ‘decomposed generic’ assumption on our maximal ideal to
instead proving results up to Tn-torsion, using our vanishing result established
in §2. Roughly speaking, saying that a maximal ideal is decomposed generic
corresponds to saying that the image of Tn in the localised Hecke algebra is a
unit.
5.2.10. Remark. We emphasise that to deduce Theorem 5.2.9 it appears
to be necessary to analyse the boundary cohomology as well.
Indeed, the
degree shifting argument only proves local-global compatibility for interior
cohomology with coefficients in specific O-local systems W (the ‘cohomolog-
ically cuspidal’ ones) which consist of only torsion classes. Moreover, even
when the Hecke eigensystem ψ of π appears in H∗
! (XK, V) for some O-local
system V with V/ϖm ∼= W/ϖm, we cannot ensure that ψ mod ϖm appears
in the image of H∗
! (XK, W) →H∗
! (XK, W/ϖm).

5.3.10. Consequences on the Galois side. — To discuss the corollary of our
description of boundary cohomology via “torsion Eisenstein series” to local-
global compatibility for the cohomology of the Borel–Serre boundary, we spe-
cialise to the setup of §5.2.5. In particular, F will now be a CM field as in
Assumption 5.2.6. We fix a finite set of finite places S of F containing Sp
and a place a ∈Sp. Moreover, we fix a dominant weight λ ∈(Zn
+)Hom(F,E), a
collection of Bernstein blocks Ω∈Q
v∈Sp B(Fv, n) and an O-integral Ω-system
L.
The following simple lemma will be used repeatedly in the proof of Lemma
5.3.12.
5.3.11. Lemma. Suppose we are given an integer h ≥1, an element T ∈TS,
a short exact sequence
0 →M′ →M →M ′′ →0
of Ta-modules of finite cardinality and ideals J′ ⊴Ta(M′), J′′ ⊴Ta(M′′)
such that, for every maximal ideal m ⊴TS with residue field k, the following
are satisfied.
1. We have (TJ′)h = 0 and (TJ′′)h = 0.
2. The TS-algebras (Ta(M′)/J′)m, (Ta(M′′)/J′′)m are of Galois type with
associated determinants D′, D′′.
3. The determinants D′, D′′ satisfy LGC(S, a, λ, Ω, L).
Then there is an ideal J ⊴Ta(M) such that (TJ)2h = 0, and for every
maximal ideal m as above, (Ta(M)/J)m satisfies (2) and (3).

5.4.10. Definition. Let eλ ∈(Z2n
+ )eIp. We say that eλ is cohomologically cus-
pidal if it satisfies the following condition: for all proper standard parabolic
subgroups Q ≤eG, for all k ∈{1, . . . , n}, and for all w ∈f
W MQ, there exist
τ, τ ′ ∈eIp such that
(w · eλ)τ,2n −(w · eλ)τ,k ̸= (w · eλ)τ ′,2n −(w · eλ)τ ′,k.
Here f
W = W((ResF +/Q eG)E, (ResF +/Q eT)E) denotes the absolute Weyl
group of the reductive group ResF +/Q eG over Q. If Q < eG is a proper standard
parabolic subgroup, then there is an isomorphism
MQ ∼= ResF/F + GLn1 × ResF/F + GLn2 × · · · × ResF/F + GLnr−1 × eGnr,
where eGnr is a quasi-split unitary group in 2nr variables. The point of the
definition is that a cuspidal, regular algebraic automorphic representation σ =
σ1⊗· · ·⊗σr of MQ(AF +) cannot have weight w·eλ, since σ1 would have weight
λ ∈(Zn1
+ )Hom(F,E) given by
λτ,i = (w · eλ)τ,2n−n1+i,
λτc,i = −(w · eλ)τ,n1+1−i,
for i = 1, . . . , n1 and τ ∈eIp. This contradicts Clozel’s purity lemma, which
implies that λτ,n1 + λτc,1 = (w · eλ)τ,2n −(w · eλ)τ,n1 is an integer independent
of τ. Therefore, any regular algebraic automorphic representation of eG(AF +)
of cohomologically cuspidal weight eλ is cuspidal.

and [Hev24, Proposition 6.5]. After combining it with the base change result
[Shi14], reciprocity for conjugate self-dual regular algebraic cuspidal automor-
phic representations of GL2n,F (see for instance [ACC+23, Theorem 2.3.3])
and the main characteristic 0 result of [Hev24] on P-ordinary local-global
compatibility cf. [Hev24, Theorem 4.27] we obtain the following theorem.

6. Remarks on Weil–Deligne representations
In this section, we prove some simple results about Weil–Deligne represen-
tations, including Corollary 6.0.6, which shows that Theorem 1.2.1 implies
Corollary 1.2.2. Our approach is inspired by the proof of [A’C24, Lemma
3.1.9].
We work over C, and consider finite-dimensional Weil–Deligne representa-
tions of WK, where K is a fixed non-archimedean local field. Let I denote the
set of isomorphism classes of irreducible continuous representations of WK on
finite-dimensional C-vector spaces, and let W denote the set of equivalence
classes in I under the relation of unramified twist. If ω ∈W, and (r, N) is a
Weil–Deligne representation on V , then the ω-isotypic part V [ω] ≤V is, by
definition, the subspace generated by WK-subrepresentations of V that lie in
ω. It is preserved by N, so defines a sub-Weil–Deligne representation of V .
6.0.1. Definition. Let (r, N) be a Frobenius-semisimple Weil–Deligne repre-
sentation on the finite-dimensional C-vector space V . We define integers
m1,ω(r, N) ≥m2,ω(r, N) ≥· · · ≥0
by the requirement that there is an isomorphism
V [ω] ∼= ⊕si∈ωsi ⊗Sp(mi,ω(r, N)).
The following is [Var24, Definition 8.2].
6.0.2. Definition. Let (r, N), (r′, N′) be Frobenius-semisimple Weil–Deligne
representations. We write (r, N) ≺(r′, N′) if for all ω ∈W, and for all i ≥1,
we have
m1,ω(r, N) + · · · + mi,ω(r, N) ≤m1,ω(r′, N′) + · · · + mi,ω(r′, N′).
Given a continuous semisimple representation r : WK →GL(V ) on a finite-
dimensional C-vector space, we define (following [Vog93]) P(r) ≤End(V ) to
be the subspace of endomorphisms N : V →V such that (r, N) is a Weil–
Deligne representation; equivalently, P(r) = HomWK(V, V (−1)).
Let G(r) = CentGL(V )(r(WK)). Then G(r) is a (connected) reductive group,
and it acts on P(r).
6.0.3. Lemma. Let (r, N), (r, N ′) be Frobenius-semisimple Weil–Deligne rep-
resentations on the finite-dimensional C-vector space V . Suppose that N lies
in the closure of the G(r)-orbit of N′. Then (r, N) ≺(r, N′).
Proof. — If ω ∈W, let r[ω] denote the representation of the Weil group on
V [ω]. Then P(r) = Q
ω P(r[ω]) and G(r) = Q
ω G(r[ω]) (and these product
decompositions are compatible). If N lies in the closure of the G(r)-orbit of
N′, then for any ω ∈W, N[ω] ∈P(r[ω]) lies in the closure of the G(r[ω])-orbit
of N′[ω], and therefore in the closure of the GL(V [ω])-orbit of N′[ω].


## Page 112


112
L. A’CAMPO, B. HEVESI, J. A. THORNE & D. WHITMORE
It is well-known (cf. [BC09, §7.8.1]) that if M, M′ are nilpotent endomor-
phisms on the finite-dimensional C-vector space W, with Jordan blocks of size
t1 ≥t2 ≥. . . and t′
1 ≥t′
2 ≥. . . , then M is in the closure of the GL(W)-orbit
of M′ if and only if for all i ≥1 we have
t1 + t2 + · · · + ti ≤t′
1 + t′
2 + · · · + t′
i.
The Jordan blocks of N[ω] have size m1,ω(r, N), . . . , m1,ω(r, N) (dim s1 times),
m2,ω(r, N), . . . , m2,ω(r, N) (dim s2 = dim s1 times), and so on. We see that we
must have (r, N) ≺(r, N′), as required.
6.0.4. Definition. Let (r, N) be a Frobenius-semisimple Weil–Deligne repre-
sentation on the finite-dimensional C-vector space V . We say that (r, N) is
generic if HomWD((r, N), (r(1), N)) = 0.
6.0.5. Proposition. Let V be a finite-dimensional C-vector space, and let
r : WK →GL(V ) be a continuous semisimple representation. Then:
1. G(r) has finitely many orbits in P(r). In particular, there is a unique
open orbit.
2. If N ∈P(r), then N lies in the unique open orbit if and only if (r, N) is
a generic Weil–Deligne representation.
3. Suppose that N, N′ ∈P(r) and that (r, N′) is a generic Weil–Deligne
representation. Then (r, N) ≺(r, N′).
Proof. — The first part is [Vog93, Proposition 4.5(4)].
For the sec-
ond, we observe that the generic locus (as the locus where the map
ad N : HomWK(r, r(1)) →EndWK(V ) is injective) is open in P(r).
It is
also non-empty, and forms a single G(r)-orbit, by [A’C24, Lemma 3.1.9].
We conclude that the unique open orbit in P(r) consists precisely of those N
such that (r, N) is generic. For the third, we observe that the first and second
parts show that N is in the closure of the generic orbit; we can then apply
Lemma 6.0.3.
6.0.6. Corollary. Let n ≥1, let π be a generic irreducible admissible
C[GLn(K)]-module, and let (r, N) be a Weil–Deligne representation on Cn
such that (r, N)ss ∼= recK(π)ss. Then (r, N)F-ss ≺recK(π).
Proof. — By [All16, Lemma 1.1.3], the genericity of π is equivalent to that
of recK(π). The result therefore follows from Proposition 6.0.5.
